--- Page 7 ---
### Hermes, Crowned with Light <note>original: "phōz" or "phōs," Greek for light</note>

> Light says: It is true, without falsehood, and most true, that what is above is like what is below, and what is below is like what is above, for the accomplishment of the miracles of the one thing. And just as all things came from one, by the meditation of the one <gloss>that is, God</gloss>, so all things were born from this one thing by adaptation. Its father is the Sun; its mother is the Moon. The wind carried it in its belly. Its nurse is the Earth. The father of all perfection <term>Thelesmi: a term for the "will" or "perfection" of the Great Work in alchemy</term> of the whole world is here. Its power is complete if it be turned into earth. You shall separate the earth from the fire, the dense from the subtle, gently and with great ingenuity. It ascends from the earth to the heaven, and again it descends into the earth, and receives the power of the higher and the lower things. Thereafter, you shall have the glory of the whole world. Therefore, all obscurity shall flee from you. This is the strong fortitude of all fortitude, because it shall conquer every subtle thing and penetrate every solid thing. Thus was the world created. From this shall come wonderful adaptations, the method of which is this. Therefore, I am called Hermes Trismegistus <note>meaning "Thrice-Greatest Hermes"</note>, having the three parts of the wisdom of the whole world. What I have said concerning the operation of the Sun and the Moon is complete.

<margin>Library</margin>
¶ The application <gloss>of these words</gloss> to the aforementioned letters, in which he shows that he possesses the whole truth <gloss>thus</gloss> <unclear>for</unclear> the one thing through which the altar of the knowledge of God <unclear>v...</unclear> [...] First, he speaks according to the opinion of those who say that by Art <unclear>f... m...</unclear> [...] is [...] experience, because experience is from <unclear>most true</unclear> [...] most true through <unclear>c... v...</unclear> he says most true in <unclear>r... p... s... q... d...c.</unclear> because the Sun <unclear>thus</unclear> <unclear>exceeds</unclear> the natural wheel, tempering <unclear>n... p... n... u</unclear> and in medicinal <unclear>matters</unclear> <unclear>with</unclear> others. Here ends the touch [...] operation.

<image-desc>Circular ink stamp of the "VATICAN APOSTOLIC LIBRARY" located at the bottom left, featuring the papal tiara and crossed keys of St. Peter.</image-desc>

<summary>This page contains a Latin transcription of the famous "Emerald Tablet" of Hermes Trismegistus, a foundational text of Western alchemy describing the relationship between the macrocosm and microcosm. The lower section includes a fragmentary commentary attempting to apply these hermetic principles to practical or medicinal "operations."</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Emerald Tablet, alchemy, As above so below, Sun and Moon, Great Work, Vatican Library, occult philosophy</keywords>

of the stone, he says. **And from the superior** 3. **so that which is in the inferior** <note>original: "sic quod est inferius" - a core tenet of the Emerald Tablet: "As above, so below."</note> first, because by this the stone is divided into two principal parts through the mastery <note>The "magisterium" or the alchemical process</note>, namely, into a superior part which ascends upwards, and into an inferior part which remains fixed and settled. And in this [latter] part are the things to be perfected, as <gloss>GEBER</gloss> <note>Referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, the famous 8th-century Islamic alchemist</note> says; and therefore he says **that which is in the superior shall become that which is in the inferior**. <unclear>He falls</unclear> **because in this division of the author for penetrating the worldly** things of the one through the stone, which now for the time of the whole [work] is called the gift and the consummation. Thus the confused mass **by the meditation of the one**, namely, it is necessary while **and just as there was one thing, all things [arose] from this thing**; namely, that all things were in nature, that is, they went out from this confused mass **by one adaptation**, namely, by the sole command of God for the world. Make the stone, that is, it is born and issuing from one confused mass, at the time holding the golden hour, which mass was created by God. And by his sole world-command, the stone is born in nature. And **so that which is born [is] one** final [thing]. It is generated more by the sun [and] the moon. But Artificially, with nature mediating, **the sun is generated by the sun with a multiplier in the root of the stone produced**. **By God it follows: its father is the Sun, its mother is the Moon** <note>A famous allegorical description of the chemical components: the Sun represents Gold/Sulfur, and the Moon represents Silver/Mercury</note>; namely, through the rule of natural generation, because the smoke's suitable receptacle is with that which ascends and then [goes] within to the father, **that is, by Artificial guidance**. It is necessary that the sun has its smoke, which is then its suitable receptacle, and it produces a response with a certain fullness to the Autumn and <unclear>others</unclear>.

And if tutty were given, it would not cover anything except what it was itself, because it would not be receptive to the tincture. For truly, a similar skill is required to have a medicine that flows. Since it puts quicksilver <note>original: "mercury vivum"</note> to flight, the union of these two is necessary; when they have received each other, then by their union they call the stone itself "the stone in the belly" <unclear>...</unclear> because it can be given.

<meta>The text continues the commentary on the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus. Having previously discussed the "Father" and "Mother" of the Stone, the scribe now moves to the operation of the fire and the cycle of ascent and descent between earth and heaven.</meta>

<margin>note</margin>
**From the fire it is born. From the spirit with great ingenuity and sweat** <note>original: "sudore"; in alchemy, "sweat" refers to the condensation or moisture released by a substance when heated</note> **and little by little, not by violence, but with ingenuity, as if in the fire, he** <unclear>personally</unclear> **shall separate and dissolve it; which dissolution is the first separator.**

<margin>note</margin>
Thirdly, it says: "From the fire, the spirit, and the smoke <note>original: "fūrem" / "fumum"</note> and the multitude." It is said "From the fire" because in such a stone, through the fire, the stone is purified. The most pure <term>super-pendent</term> <note>a term likely referring to the weight or refinement of the substance</note> by this act; thus a suitable stone is made for multiplying <note>original: "multiplicadū"; multiplication is the alchemical process of increasing the quantity or potency of the Philosopher's Stone</note> and one part is multiplied to a stable state, melted in the tooth <note>likely referring to a "dent" or crucible</note>, impossible in its hardness, yet divided among the soft things. **It ascends, however, from the earth to heaven and again it descends into the earth.** <note>original: "Ascendit autem de terra in celum et iterum descendit in terram"; a core axiom of the Emerald Tablet describing the circulation of matter</note> This is a very notable thing, because while they are in the stone in the first operation, they are <unclear>joined</unclear>...

<margin>note note</margin>
<unclear>...</unclear> the parts that exist. And what else is here, in our time, it hardens.

<margin>note</margin>
This <unclear>God</unclear> is above; there are two principal parts, or rather one that is called

<margin>note note</margin>
"ascending above," which is the <term>non-fixed</term> <note>original: "non fixa"; the volatile part of the substance that evaporates or turns to gas</note> and the other part is that which remains <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "fixa"; the solid residue that resists the fire</note>, which is called the <term>ferment</term> <note>original: "fimentu" / "fermentum"</note>, which nourishes the whole stone and firms it.

<margin>note</margin>
As is to be understood in this regarding the <unclear>percussion</unclear>, namely the parts which by themselves

<margin>note note</margin>
in the fire are understood; but in time it makes the part harden. Regarding that non-fixed part, it is a good <term>Azoth</term> <note>original: "az"; Azoth is the essential agent of transformation in alchemy, often symbolized as mercury</note> which it retains, or it deceives and they give the stone which is the most pure weight. Through the <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the successful completion of the alchemical process</note> they give the whole stone until the non-fixed parts are defended and stood up; and this is what is said: **It ascends, however, from the earth to heaven.** After the stone itself is thus exalted, all <unclear>darkness</unclear> <note>original: "tendare" / "tenebrae"</note> above it dies, when it is entirely drawn out from the stone in the operation. Which is called the stone. and by roasting and touching, they give the <unclear>tooth</unclear> <note>crucible</note> and permit it to live with the common things, to exalt the whole stone; **again it descends into the earth** and it is purified, fixed, and flowed.

<summary>This page provides a technical commentary on the "Ascent and Descent" phase of the Emerald Tablet, explaining how heat (fire) separates the volatile "spirit" from the "fixed" body of the Stone. It emphasizes that this process must be done "little by little" with skill rather than force to achieve the "Mastery."</summary>

<meta>The text continues the commentary on the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, specifically interpreting the lines "It receives the force of things above and below" and "Thus you will have the glory of the whole world." The author links the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone to the biblical/philosophical creation of the cosmos from a single "mass."</meta>

It is that which, and the same as that which, defends the earth and thus receives a stronger force and power by defending it; because the corporeal, through steadfastness <note>original: "fide"; likely referring to the fixation or stabilization of a substance</note>, becomes spiritual by will. And because the spiritual and the corporeal [interact] through steadfastness by defending...

---

It possesses its own glory, the whole world in one stone thus opened outside this possible world. <insert>VATICAN APOSTOLIC LIBRARY</insert> All darkness shall flee from it <note>original: "Fugiet are ore obscuritatē"; a variation of the Emerald Tablet's "Fugiet a te omnis obscuritas," meaning ignorance or impurity will be dispelled</note>. Say that the stone and poverty do not dwell together, because the stone thus opened conquers all things by its action. That is, the whole is **Stronger than Strength itself** <note>original: "Forti' Fortitudine"; referring to the Stone's "superiority over every subtle thing"</note>. For no one [can compare] any other strength of this world to the power of this stone, because it has taken all powers into itself so that it may penetrate every subtle thing; that is, it conquers and, by conquering, enters into every thing that exists. And when it divides and dissolves another thing, it penetrates it. It follows:

---

<margin>110 110</margin>
**Just as the world was created.** <margin>note note</margin> That is, the Philosopher <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary author of the Emerald Tablet</note> gives an example regarding the portion of this stone, saying: "thus the world was created," because there was one stone which in the beginning was the whole world. And because there is in the world one fused mass—not a sign through itself, but by the right hand, command, or nod of God—He spoke into the mass so that it might be mutably varied into the four elements. And wisely, through the species, they were divided; this happened through the various species being opened from one another. Now, because they are divided by separation and from different "cells," they were from that point more clearly wonderful. That is, if the separation was so great, now they shrink from the open proportions; now those who, in the operation of our stone, look back upon the joining of those elements in these open miracles...

---

<margin>110 110</margin>
<margin>note note</margin> Also this is the sign that it even <margin>note note</margin> thus seems, however, of this kind, etc. Because now the Philosopher has given the proportion of how the stone should be, he provides a second way concerning how there may be one stone in which the spirit is noted, like a son coming to this, his fatherland, from his own name <note>the "fatherland" here likely refers to the original state of the matter or its final, perfected home</note>.

<summary>This page provides a metaphysical commentary on the Emerald Tablet, comparing the alchemical creation of the Philosopher's Stone to the divine creation of the four elements from a single primordial mass. It emphasizes the Stone's "stronger strength" and its ability to penetrate and conquer all matter.</summary>

<insert>1 — they might give <unclear>rere</unclear></insert> just as [was said] before. From this root, they say concerning that part: **"the three parts of the wisdom of this world"** <note>original: "tres mundi p^t^es sapie hui^a^"; this refers to the title "Trismegistus" or Thrice-Great</note> and all the matters which are in the world and [the spirits].
<margin>man</margin> This is why I say one principle has [three] parts, because it follows the division of the world, which the "Bird of Hermes" <note>original: "auis p^l^is"; likely *avis palladis* or *avis hermetis*, a symbol for the volatile spirit</note> says has three principal parts. These he calls the three parts of the wisdom of the whole world. <insert>as</insert> The first part is mundane <note>mineral</note>, vegetable, and animal; in these, the <unclear>universal</unclear> is joined. Concerning these, the philosophers hold that they possess spirit and body, as well as the operation of the sun and moon <note>Alchemical gold and silver</note>. And thus it is said, **"the three parts of wisdom belong to this man,"** because he says these three parts are joined in a single stone in every way. And therefore it is called a "precious stone," because it has the nature of the mundane, the vegetable, and the animal kingdoms. And it is one stone having [many] colors, because the second and all [parts] are said to be therein. 
This was not a "dead" thing, for it is the very hand of the sun; and even if it was "dead"—as was said above—it was led through a cycle with the <unclear>fixation</unclear> of blood until it was finished. This is evidently the fulfillment of the operation of the aforementioned things.

The first place: if you thus prepare the stone of the universe as I have said, and if you do not know where I said it, <margin>not so</margin> <insert>and the stone</insert> then the third [is like] the second. It is said to be filled by the three, as has been said concerning the operation of the stone and its nature. The second [nature] is hot, as said in the third part, and it refers all things into one. Only in this way is it a "Vegetable Stone," <term>Vegetable Stone<gloss>An alchemical substance derived from organic matter (like wine or plants) rather than minerals, believed to have particular life-giving properties.</gloss></term> through that which I speak. From the fruits of the books of the Lord, through the two metals where they are common, [it is] particularly the greatest. It is seen to become "The White" <note>original: "Albu^m^"; the *Albedo* or whitening stage of the alchemical process</note> and is celebrated. 

After this was written in the year [14]20, it was said that much must be amended, which in truth was said in this manner: that the Vegetable Stone is three [in one] and he himself says he knows the second way of which I speak. He found it beyond himself, for after the melting of the <unclear>multitude</unclear>, another was added to another by the mode of a part of the vapor of life. Gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> is the perfection of metals, and it is that redness which fixes <note>In alchemy, "fixation" is the process by which a volatile substance is rendered stable and resistant to fire.</note> and supports every body with which it is mixed by itself; it fixes through its own great ingenuity, which does not perish to the artisan, of whom it is said—as much as <unclear>glidire</unclear> is said to be—it was. Silver <note>original: "Luna"</note> is a pure whiteness, and although it is said to be of <unclear>itself</unclear>, yet as it moves toward and mixes by itself, it does not fix. But in its own nature, through its own artifice, nature knows how to have one thing more [than another], and through that it is held in perfection; it fixes by itself and by its own [nature], through which it appears more [clearly] in the fire, and then it does not leave. 

But concerning its state, it is said that if <unclear>God</unclear> <note>original: "deus"</note> the father should consider it, it says much in its own nature. Two things approach each other, but they are different; it is said one is called "empty," as no certain act has been done, which, if placed through vapor, is given by a cause with the first evacuation of silver <note>original: "argenti"</note> having been made, and it did not reveal itself in weight in that manner. It is redeemed in the name through which the whole [work] is deigned in the Mastery <term>Magisterium; the completion of the alchemical Great Work</term>. This now gives that which is the pure [essence] of Gold and Silver, and through such a thing nature is joined to both. 

Sometimes it happens that there is a good evacuation, and because the labor concerning Gold is beyond measure, it nevertheless establishes a great profit in the root. Thus, in the affinity of Gold and Silver, then more [than ever] as Gold is known, so it fixes itself because of the state between Gold and Silver; and that happens from it in the operation, for by such a thing it recedes, adds redness <note>original: "rubedinis"</note>, and shines in that place with an inestimable brilliance, and the weight rules in the Mastery which otherwise would not. And [it is] pure in such a white state; he who was able to complete its way of Gold could take it from its changeable persons. 

It leads into Gold and Silver and finds from it <insert>always through</insert> the nature of [that which] joins Gold and Silver; it is not found by them through ancient things, yet it prevails in the Mastery. But he finds it because he did not find the artifice of Gold, through which it was red; by such a whiteness it is not distracted, and thus it joins, the white color not having been changed from before, but increasing it in that which all such riches remain—their more splendid and clearer brightness.

# Toward the principal point, let there be seen by mercy the **definition of this gift or art, and the definition can be defined.**
It is nothing else than an art admitting or inspiring all the sacred [ways] of metals, which through the form of the heart moves [them] toward a natural fulfillment; it leads [them] naturally [down]. And in this reasoning properly and more [fully], for if [the matter] ought to be moved and proportioned toward one another, having been brought through to that which generates gold, and so for other <margin>number</margin> common minerals. He who has attained its perfect gifts is full of grace, with the best powers and riches. It begins, but it must not be hastened to order its work from the masters, as the <term>Philosopher</term> <note>original: "phō"; likely referring to Aristotle, often cited as "The Philosopher" in medieval texts</note> says: because every age holds nothing in no time, because it is given in a smaller or larger work so that it may be performed.

It is gathered in the earth or from all seeds that have been and are [found], which with the water of the two elixirs gives the ferment; and let the paste of the ferment be made. It is said that they are the ferment from it, and when the ferment is sought, it is nothing but a paste; then the light of the ferment is nothing but the body. And this is the **Key of the Philosophers** <note>original: "clauis phōz"</note> and of all the wise. It is said that before the <unclear>philosopher</unclear> can act, the action of the redness is in the fixed heat of the ferment, and it increases in whatever mineral the color is lighter or higher. From this, the master is able to see that through the stone they can pursue the art and the form, and in the examination, the rest of the words are not [needed].

### Concerning the Stone of the Philosophers, which is called *indasengu*, concerning its matter and its nature and...

**I**t is found oily and cold, and its "eyes" <note>original: "oculi"; likely referring to the crystalline facets or shiny spots in the mineral</note> are where elsewhere it <unclear>overcomes</unclear>, because the reason for its appearance is near to all perfect bodies. **O**ur **Stone of the Philosophers**, which is *indasengu* <note>original: "indasengu"; this appears to be a corruption of a technical alchemical term, possibly of Arabic origin or a specific name for the prima materia</note> according to **Plato**, is described as such: because in its manifest state it is oily/fatty and... they destroy all matters. For it is everything above **whiteness** <note>original: "albedinem"</note> and **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem"</note>. It is the water that is perfected: the **living water** and **dead water**, the **virgin's milk** <note>original: "lac virginis"; a common term for mercurial water or a white precipitate</note>, the water of washing, and the **Aralian fountain** <note>original: "fons aralis"</note>. Of this fountain, he who drinks shall not die for eternity. It is the perceiver of colors—as I say, of perfect bodies and colors. And it is that which vivifies and **mortifies** <note>original: "mortificat"; kills or fixes a substance</note>, dries and moistens, heats and <unclear>gives</unclear>, and causes it to become dry according to the measure of its rule. 

And know that it is a "living opacity," and that which is dead is different from the other. That which is above the fluities is different, and that which is dissolved is different from the harsher opacities. God, who [poured] the mind upon it and purges it, and through His index and His wine <note>or "vineyard"</note> grants grace to it; and by the aforementioned, the truth is loved and it becomes clear. It dissipates over time; when the fire is able to be upon it, it does not compel it, but it flees from it... which is a gift of the angels for the wise philosophers to know. One must wait gradually with the fire <unclear>so that</unclear> it does not do damage to the rule, and it is driven away from it so that when it is fixed, it may cast itself upon something between both wonderful and changed...

### The Principles of the Art

¶ First, it must be known concerning all those things which are necessary for this art, and concerning its actions, for anyone who wishes to know them in brief. It must be noted, therefore, that in every part of this work, certain substances <note>original: "p. alixia rerum"; possibly referring to preparations or elixirs</note> are necessary; namely, that there be oil, vinegar, and a body upon which the aforementioned must be sprinkled. And now he will speak by their numbers, that is, of the things themselves. 

**Lime** <note>original: "Calx"; in alchemy, the "calx" is the fine powder left after a substance has been heated or oxidized, representing the "death" of the old form</note> is indeed the visible powder of clean things, soluble except for those divided which are placed upon a swift divider. Just as there is white water which is in the sun's ray and they are white. But the cause is red. Like blood. 

**Water** is their soluble liquid, fleeting and cleansing the body, which, as if by wood, fixes [it] in the fire in the earth of their root. And the skillful [artificer] says that it is water that is not wet <note>original: "aqua non humida"; a classic alchemical paradox referring to a "dry" or metallic liquid, often mercury</note>, fat/oily and leading. ### The Seven Actions

The wise man indeed wishes to work in this part; he who has not changed the substance of the unknown things does not scorn to descend to one of the work's aforementioned actions. Because in a multitude of other things, you will never find the end and the principal thing. The actions are seven, namely: 
1. <term>Solution</term> <note>original: "solutio"; the process of dissolving a solid into liquid</note>

3. <term>Distillation</term> <note>original: "distillatio"; purifying a liquid by boiling and condensing it</note>

5. <term>Congealing</term> <note>original: "congelatio"; making a liquid solid again</note>
6. <unclear>Marriage</unclear> <note>original: "maridatio"; likely "maritatio," the alchemical "wedding" or union of opposing principles</note>
7. <term>Fixation</term> <note>original: "fixio"; making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand high heat without evaporating</note>

For, by God, you who seek this teaching: We hand this to you along with our other writings. Indeed, no method is found to be as easy in them as this one. Accordingly, we shall explain the order of this book out of twenty [parts]. We set forth this book in this order, which is not found in others; for no one else produces the medicine through such a sequence, and it pleased us to travel through the entire order. Therefore, we say first: Let it now be known to you that this first mode is **distillation** <note>original: "distillacionem"</note>, which is the separation of the humors <note>The liquid parts or "moistures" of a substance.</note> and the dry parts from one another, and this is our "first and third" <note>This likely refers to the first and third stages of the Great Work or the relationship between the elements.</note>. Now, this distillation is done so that you place the stones in a **cucurbit** <note>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still.</note> and a pot in which there is a division, under which the dense water may be separated, and the water may go forth so that [the vessel] does not cause it to fall back in. After this, however, [proceed] regarding the distilled water again in the same manner <note>original: "viceprovite"; possibly a corruption of "vice versa" or "provide."</note> entirely. This truly happens when the distilled water is with another [substance]; let it fall from itself into it in such a way that something remains so that the water is not diminished. Secondly, indeed, after the water has been distilled, let it undergo a stronger fire, and then the air purifies the distillation, [lest] it fail from the distilling. And when it has been distilled, then take it and [act] strongly so that you place the water from the highest part of the dregs. Likewise, let the cucurbit close it until it returns in three or four [cycles]. And this is the first order in this book.

### ¶ The Solution

The method of **projection** <note>original: "piciendi" for "proiciendi"; the final stage of alchemy where the "philosopher's stone" or elixir is cast upon a base metal to transform it into gold or silver</note> is as follows:
You should project one part of this **elixir** <note>A medicinal or transformative substance sought by alchemists</note> upon a thousand parts of proven [metal] <note>original: "p[r]o[b]"; referring to "tested" or purified metal</note>. If you then project one part of that proven metal upon a thousand parts again... and so on upon a thousand; and you shall do this until you have made a great quantity. Thus ends the **Book of Thirty Flowers.** <note>original: "Expliciat lib[er] xxx. floru[m]."</note>

### The Letter of the Philosopher Abu Ali ibn Sina (Avicenna) to Hasen regarding the "Right Matter," sent for the joy of friends

<margin>
The Letter of the philosopher Abu Ali ibn Sina (Avicenna) to Hasen regarding the "Right Matter," sent for the joy of friends.
</margin>

You have asked of me, my dear Hasen <note>original: "m[i] husen"; likely Abu al-Hasan, a frequent interlocutor in works attributed to Avicenna</note>, something concerning that which you yourself have done... so that it may be understood and held in memory. And that I might explain with what methods and ingenuity I applied myself to that end, based on what I have known...

<margin>Greetings</margin>
Know truly that in natural things we work toward a supreme end, which the mother of **alchemy** <note>original: "alchimia"</note> brings to those who are perfecting the work. It is one of those arts by which he who desires the good and the sought-after end may rejoice. And the "bodies" <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to the seven metals (gold, silver, mercury, copper, iron, tin, lead)</note> are "healed" <note>original: "sanantur"; alchemists often spoke of base metals as being "sick" and the elixir as a medicine that "heals" them into gold</note>... this work was not done by my art alone, which sounds the harmony of both things. I have therefore considered the books written affirmatively on the art, and I found them empty of the reasons which ought to be present in their authors; and thus I found much in them that was erroneous... I have set [the truth] down in this book... And always maintain the due reverence and honor. May the power of the Lord not be lacking to us. Returning to me, I have considered and begun to meditate. I say to you: the matter is that which is not, and yet is. The form of life is possible for us.

<summary>This page marks the conclusion of the "Book of Thirty Flowers" with instructions on the "projection" of the elixir to multiply its transformative power, followed by the opening of a letter attributed to Avicenna (Abu Ali ibn Sina) regarding the true principles of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Abu Ali, Hasen, Book of Thirty Flowers, projection, elixir, sublimation, cucurbit, alchemy, Epistola de Re Recta</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter of Avicenna (Abu Ali ibn Sina) to Hasen, moving from the initial greeting into the technical requirements for the "Great Work." It discusses the stability of tinctures and the specific roles of sulfur and arsenic in the transmutation of metals.</meta>

...so that silver may be transmuted into the color of gold, and as **bronze** <note>original: "es"; refers to copper or its alloys like bronze</note> into the color of silver, which is neither from our red-making **tincture** <note>A liquid or solid substance used by alchemists to "stain" or transform the color and nature of a base metal</note> nor from our white-making [tincture]. It must be known that the mixing of the tincture with hard, stony bodies is not possible unless they are first washed and the bodies are made smooth <note>original: "leificent"; likely from "levigare," meaning to polish or make smooth</note>, and this is not possible except through the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the catalyst of alchemical change</note>.

In the Stone, however, what is now called the **red** or **white tincture** allows us to color things at the opportune time. This must be done so that the tincture does not produce a red color by burning away in the fire; or, if it does not burn, that it does not flee [vaporize], but rather if it escapes at the end, it does not work. Or if, by not burning, it does not violate [the metal] but is not consumed nor **perfected** <note>original: "perimfacitur"; likely a scribal variant of "perficitur," meaning to complete or perfect the chemical process</note>, it is of no use. 

Or if, when it is consumed and perfected in this way, it does not remain on the **assay-scale** <note>original: "axe deponis"; referring to the balance or the testing of the weight</note> but instead purifies and flies away, and separates from the preparation, then it [the work] fails. Or if this separated part does not fly away but does not produce silver, then it is merely burnable by those things which do not burn gold, but burn silver—namely **sulfur** and the other things by which gold is purified from silver. Thus, it would not then produce **bronze** and so on, because it does not burn away from those things which do not burn silver, but it does burn bronze and other things more than the rest. 

For there is a need within the tincture for the tincture to be required. However, the **white tincture**, which is mixed into stony bodies so that it does not burn when in the presence of sharp, corrosive things, remains entirely purged and does not allow the bodies to be burned during the separation. 

It is clear that these operations occur from middle-grade simple substances through such means, and these words concerning proper and middle-grade things have reached us as a **medicine** <note>The alchemical elixir was often called a "medicine" for metals, "healing" them into gold</note>. Look at this impression which has appeared to us concerning the invention <unclear>of the work</unclear>, and then the narration of these things: namely, that **red sulfur** serves for the experiment in silver, and **white arsenic** serves for these operations. 

From this, it is estimated that this is the truth. Thus, it does not burn gold or anything else, because the wise men were present; others, acting as mediators, narrate such things. Therefore, it is necessary for us to find this operation through our own **mastery** <note>original: "magisterium"; the perfected skill or the resulting substance of the Great Work</note>.

We have found, indeed, that in the tincture which entirely embraces [the metal], there is **lead**, which provides for the lusters; and lead solidifies **life** <note>An alchemical metaphor, likely referring to the "spirit" or volatile part of the substance</note>. And lead coagulates, and once life is joined and fixed over the fire, it leads the silver to be perfected...

<summary>Avicenna discusses the requirements for a stable alchemical tincture, emphasizing that it must not burn away or evaporate during the heating process. He identifies the specific roles of red sulfur and white arsenic in transforming silver and bronze.</summary>
<keywords>tincture, sulfur, arsenic, lead, transmutation, gold, silver, bronze, Philosopher's Stone, fire stability</keywords>

# Chapter 2: Concerning the Tincture of that Mercury

<margin>Chapter 2: Concerning the tincture of mercury</margin>

**C**oncerning the white [tincture], which in a certain way colors **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury</note> to a pure white. And let it be colored with its whitening <note>original: "allufactoe"; likely referring to the process of "albefaction" or turning a substance white</note> so that it adheres to the [metallic] bodies and penetrates into the spirits, and with its abundance, let it be **decocted** <note>original: "decoquere"; to boil down or concentrate a substance using heat</note> in vinegar with its medicines. Without delay, the whiteness penetrates into the midst, and it becomes according to the measures of silver in whiteness; and so that the middle part, when the quicksilver is increased by the **regimen** <note>The "regimen" is the specific set of rules or stages of heat and timing in the alchemical process</note>, gives us the impression and work [...]

It is written that the tincture of mercury flees from the fire <note>It is volatile and evaporates when heated</note> and is separated from the mixture, and is not mixed except with those things which can be placed together peacefully. But since it has this goodness, it is not burned upon the fire, but is only heated by it; for it is excused from the labor of burning. And the iron [or vessel] that is with the spirit in it, is breathed upon so that in the presence of the liquid it is pressed into a white state according to its disposition.

Therefore, secondly, we must consider the computation of this thing and the **distillation** <note>The process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and then condensing it back into a liquid</note> of the waters from it; so that it heats, or is pulverized, or flees, or is held, so that with the "foot and column" <note>likely referring to the base and the neck of the distilling apparatus, such as an alembic</note> it holds the spirit with the materials and mixes it with them [...] it is killed, and thus a reception occurs in which an immutable state and waters perhaps happen; that **menstruum** <note>A solvent, often called "the philosophical vinegar," used to dissolve solids</note> which is with us and which we cannot do without, and they desire constant mixing. And there are steps so that we decoct the mercury upon the fire again, so that all [impurities] are separated from it, and it remains in such a state that no further change is possible, so that what is volatile evaporates from it. From the residue, whether it be in a temperate heat or in a greater heat, it appears nearly like the earth, to the end that I might mix with it the middle parts which I cannot separate from it. And according to the regimen, let us take the moisture from the [...] and let us distill.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the "Rubedo" or red stage, specifically focusing on the management of heat and moisture. And by an <unclear>intense</unclear> fire, let its dregs <term>dregs: the sediment or solid residue, often called "feces" in older texts, left after distillation or burning</term> or ash remain. Some say that regarding this, just as the drynesses indicated are combined in our [substances], even another species [is added].

But experience testifies in the book: And it is that this [substance] does not burn the <unclear>people</unclear>, rather in the first stage, when the color is cast upon it, it stands before the burning; it is hidden. Others say it is not in its nature to evaporate in such a way that it remains dry; the tinting substance does not burn from it to itself in liquefied bodies. Furthermore, as it appears in the flesh of plants, it burned in that which is the heat of a lively fire by impacts. And know that not only, but through the dryness of the mixture with the dry and the whole animal substance, it is not evacuated of its **unctuosity** <note>original: "unctuositate"; the oily, fatty, or flammable principle in a substance, often associated with its soul or sulfurous quality</note>. And in one point the thought agrees: that the **humidity** in humid bodies is not evacuated by a vehement germination <note>original: "gminatione"; likely referring to the vigorous boiling or "sprouting" of the matter under heat</note> combined with the dryness in them. Thus, when the fire moves them toward vapor, it was standing, and the dregs would not separate; rather it was in it and liquefies, or it is softened gradually in its course. And if the humidity was evacuated, it dilates and evaporates, so that it is a vapor like smoke mixed with the dryness. And that which is in them that is not purified before it is united with the part.

Furthermore, through this edification <note>original: "hedificationem"; here meaning the building up or synthesis of the substance</note> with experience, it was physical. Rather, the highest dryness is fixed through **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance into a vapor so it collects as a purified solid on the upper parts of the vessel</term>. For it is certain among us that they are not corrupted with vapor, nor diminished by vapor, and that the humidity is mixed with its dryness. And when we separated [them] by working in it, we distinguished the burning from it.

And we see many parts for that purpose. For there are modes of preparation with adhering things, helpers and makers, of which one is **sublimation**. For without doubt, sublimation burns externally when it burns. And let it be known healthfully that it does not burn [the core substance]. And through this, it lacks that to which we might draw back from the burnt state, so that we may return it to the principle <note>original: "principio"; the primary, original state of the matter before it was corrupted by the fire</note>.

...the substance sublimes white, in which there is no mixture of burning <note>original: "adustionis"; the process of scorching or chemically altering a substance through dry heat</note>. Through this, the work does not cease from the <unclear>sphere</unclear> with which we melted it and its adherence (and indeed with the mixture of it) quickly because of [its nature].

Burning, however, is when it never either burns or corrupts; this is the substance of the thing. We know that we have already <unclear>set right</unclear> that which should not burn. And it should be known that first, humidity is properly <unclear>hardened</unclear> through the seven-fold <note>original: "septie"; likely referring to the seven stages of distillation or a seven-fold purification process</note> superfluous humidity and burning humidity. And we knew through this experiment, as it discerns or distinguishes the knowledge of that which makes a thing completely white. After we found that it adheres, it appeared to us as we desired. And because it does not sublime, we distinguish it in oil or another thing while a light fire is over it and it evaporates through this superfluous humidity and is dried; and there remains from this dryness that which is like **gems in dryness** <note>likely referring to the crystalline, jewel-like residue left after the liquid has evaporated</note>. For they are not [liquid]; rather, when moved by fire, it liquefies them. It is not to be wondered at that oil is agitated by oil or water over the fire.

Furthermore, after we knew this way, we did not find **sulfur** <term>sulfur: the active, male, or flammable principle in alchemy</term> combined in the red through the white. But you have it better this way: we found **arsenic** <term>arsenic: often used in alchemy to "whiten" copper or as a reagent for the white stage</term> with the white to be better altogether, especially if this is done by anointment. Furthermore, it is possible for us to prepare the red with another goodness, even **making it red** <term>rubifacium: the process of rubification or turning the substance red</term> through things that redden it; and these are **waters of vinegars** <note>original: "aque acetorum"; acidic distillates used as solvents or reactive agents</note> and they are red-tinting agents. There is also that which is for **whitening** <term>albificandum: the process of albedo or whitening</term> another, although the point of your intention should be more in the understanding of reddening.

<margin>dissolution <unclear/></margin>
...they should be ground thoroughly with a strong lye <term>lixivia: a solution of alkaline salts, usually made from wood ashes, used as a powerful cleaning agent</term> and boiled in it with vinegar until they are dried out. Then, add bridge-water <note>original: "aqua sublicini"; the exact identity of this "water" is unclear, but it may refer to water collected near wooden bridge piles or a specific mineral water</note> and grind them with sal ammoniac <term>sale armoniaco: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy as a flux to help metals melt and react</term>, quicklime <term>calce viva: calcium oxide, known for its corrosive and heat-generating properties when wet</term>, and sea salt, and continue this until they no longer release any blackness from themselves but become cleaner; and thus, let them be evaporated over the fire. A clean substance will then be seen, until they are united with each other.

<margin>calcination</margin>
Also, take from the "goat" <note>original: "capre"; likely referring to a specific alchemical vessel with horn-like projections or a substance metaphorically named a "goat"</note> and its remaining parts a coagulation with a heat that is steady but not strong, until they thicken. Then, having taken cinnabar <term>cinabrio: mercury sulfide, a bright red mineral often used as a source of mercury</term>, boil it in the water in which the lime was previously boiled; perhaps it will give a red color. And so let it be done by them more until it whitens itself.

<margin>Chapter IX on calcination</margin>
### Chapter IX: On Calcination

And they are powdered with sal ammoniac, vinegar, and alum water <term>aqua aluminis: water containing alum, used to stabilize colors and harden substances</term> with rough burning materials until the matter is converted into a powder with salt, sal ammoniac, and sulfur. And because cinnabar works well with gold, continue for as long as it takes until the powder is drawn from them... until a powder remains. Then, let it be soaked by itself until white in distilled salt water, ground, and left with cinnabar until it dries; and let this not be done by fire until you soak it well to whiteness with ammoniac water. And as we have already said regarding the redness of sulfur or other things, there is a steady roasting until they are made ready; let a powder be made from one as if through...

<margin>Chapter X on Dissolution</margin>
### Chapter X: On Dissolution

Regarding dissolution <note>original: "resolutione"; the process of breaking a solid down into liquid or its constituent parts</note>, in the way we transmit it, dissolution is a "fire without fire" <note>original: "ignis sine igne"; a classic alchemical term for chemical reactions that generate their own heat or the use of gentle, indirect heat like a water bath</note> for things receiving dissolution through a fine herb, salt, and alum. And we have seen, according to the order of the "flesh" <note>original: "carnibus"; used here to refer to the solid pulp or essential matter of the substance</note>, sal ammoniac dissolved in water; and striving for a steady soaking of the external thing, and roasting it near heat for a long time until everything goes out into the sal ammoniac. And thus, as there is produced upon it a stone of dissolved ammoniac, grind it other times until it loses its fiery color until it is done. Also, let them place it in a bath until it takes it up thus, and let them be put together. Also, and again above it... it removes it in itself. Also, let them be soaked in it. Afterward, let us do the same again in this way, then in another. Also, let them be ground together, one upon the other, and let us do this until they are united, and so that it may catch the fire of fire. Also, let it be dissolved in water and nothing remain.

<meta>This page transitions from the practical chemical instructions of the previous page to a more theoretical and philosophical treatise. It marks the end of a previous section on the "red elixir" and the beginning of a work titled "The Book of the Perfect Mastery." The text establishes a correspondence between alchemy (referred to as "lower astronomy") and celestial astronomy, categorizing various metals and minerals based on their "fixity" or volatility when exposed to fire.</meta>

& by heat it is earth / and the white [substance] which it offers to the bones in which there is not every [substance] it will offer to the whole. [It] Ends.

## The Book of the Perfect
<margin>q</margin> **Mastery** Begins. The Book of the Perfect Mastery Begins.

**S**ince you wish [to know] the reasoning of the **mastery** <term>mastery: also known as the "magisterium," the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</term> through the investigation of skillful study, the book of the perfect mastery begins; seek the works of the art of things with diligence. [Listen] my son, with the most correct explanations of the mastery. Make a living sculpture of the deep art with [your] face [on it], which [things] that are hidden <note>original: "cacant," likely a scribal error for "latent" (hidden) or "lucent" (shining)</note> in our light they might afterwards argue. Because it [is found] to lead away according to nature. These things will not suffice because the work of art operates on the non-apparent, and we have known its manifest natures. Nevertheless, in the art of the perfect [mastery], it recognizes without error those things concerning the better and hidden natures, as read in many books of occult philosophy, and of that part of philosophy which is called **metaphysics**. For it is read of the highest soul <note>original: "aime," likely "animae"</note> concerning the extending and restraining of the forms of things and elements. Otherwise, [this is] the book which is the Great Secret.

<margin>concerning lower astronomy</margin> **K**now through this art the science of **lower astronomy** <term>lower astronomy: a synonym for alchemy, based on the belief that metals are the earthly reflections of the celestial planets</term>. Just as [higher] astronomy [speaks] through superior things. Higher astronomy speaks of the perfect stars in the firmament. (And of our earth, those which are called planets <term>planete: the seven "wandering stars" of the ancient world, each associated with a specific metal</term> which are carried by certain motions in the firmament, such as the Sun [gold], Moon [silver], Mercury [quicksilver], Venus [copper], Mars [iron], Jupiter [tin], and Saturn [lead].) In the superior [science], however, it speaks of stones fixed in the fire and of those which flee from the fire. Indeed, the stones that are called "stars" <note>in this context, "fixed" or non-volatile substances</note> are **gold**, **silver**, **lead**, **tin**, **iron**, carbuncle <note>a deep red gemstone, possibly garnet or ruby</note>, and emerald, and the remaining stones that do not flee from the fire. However, the stones that are called "planets" <note>volatile substances that evaporate or change when heated</note> are **quicksilver**, **sulfur**, **arsenic**, **sal ammoniac**, **tutia** <note>zinc oxide</note>, **magnesia**, and **marcasite** <note>original: "marcharita"; though "pearl" in classical Latin, in alchemy it often refers to marcasite or metallic minerals</note>. These do not endure upon the fire, but gradually flee from it and proceed toward the <unclear>heights</unclear>, [becoming] certain invisible things, [bound] by chains and artifice in an indissoluble bond before they show different [states]. <unclear>Reddish</unclear> <unclear>substances</unclear> are the unwearied goals of the philosophers, which the operator [learns] through the many fallacies of labors and the doubts of works; and the philosophy of the perfect [mastery] scrutinizes most perfectly and has left these things to be experienced by its sons. Not otherwise than what they bring to their own sons... to accept the process... Peter... to spare what was found.

<summary>This page introduces "The Book of the Perfect Mastery," framing alchemy as "lower astronomy" where metals correspond to planets. It distinguishes between "fixed" substances (stars) like gold and iron that survive fire, and "volatile" substances (planets) like mercury and sulfur that evaporate.</summary>

## On the Knowledge of Stones Fixed by Fire

<margin>On the knowledge of stones fixed by fire</margin>
<margin>VATICAN APOSTOLIC LIBRARY</margin>
**L**et them see how their prepared stones are placed upon the fire, and similarly [how they are acted upon] through the fire, by the fire, and through [it] (without the back, as it were) and yet do not lose the nature of their origin. Thus, by their own virtue—which lies dead—they may be quickened and alerted by their own <term>spirits: volatile substances or essences that can be extracted or released from a solid body</term>. In the work, it is thus with all its components. And this is accomplished with many years of work to bring it to perfection, since they are dug out from their own mines. And yet it is said to be done in a single day, corresponding to my works. Whence, so that God may favor it, it is done in one hour; let it be, however, with the <term>medicine: the alchemical agent or elixir used to transform base metals</term> and with the body for a long time beforehand, when they are prepared in advance. Likewise, because <term>fixed stones: substances that have been treated so they no longer evaporate or change when exposed to heat</term> by fire may pervade freely, they will happen because of it. Because the impression of heat provides for them by itself. They are earth through the stones fixed upon the fire, without anything preceding them. And by accidents they cannot [attain] their qualities—accidents not associated with smoke—by proven means. Just as stones crushed by fire cannot demonstrate their virtues unless they have been cleansed of smoke. Know here that this is more perfect.

## On the Cleansing of Bodies and Spirits

<margin>On the cleansing of bodies and spirits</margin>
**K**now that the aforementioned things do not receive each other well unless they have first been cleansed by the work. Likewise, they do not receive a permanent <term>body: the solid, material aspect of a substance</term> into a spirit until it is such that, beyond the spiritual, the corporeal is present, [and] they are [cleansed] from all filth; through which, then purified, even if it be spiritual, it may nonetheless have its own spirit through itself. Let them be pure and clean to perform the perfect work, and the more of them that is held in the mixtures, the more it is bound by the bodies upon the fire. Know here that this is more perfect.

# Here begins the second book of Galen on the elixir of the sun and the moon

The **Tree of Nature** <note>original: "Arbor physice." In alchemy, this tree often represents the developmental process of the Stone, where different "fruits" or "branches" signify stages of chemical transformation.</note>, which is called "A," varies its own branches and produces various fruits. And although this tree, while producing identical twins <note>Likely referring to the dual nature of the elixir: the "Sun" (gold) and the "Moon" (silver).</note>, perfects a most unified nature, it is nevertheless described by the cultivator in many ways through the industry which is observed and most carefully gathered.

In this tree, however, logic with all its fervor yields the first fruit <unclear>m' m' et</unclear> and its interior is illustrated with eloquent colors; indeed, it offers within it the first <unclear>product</unclear> of twin fruits, and everything is made that supports the divine for the wise. Finally, one perceives apples <unclear>of its own</unclear> in the same tree, which illuminated the <unclear>vision</unclear> with various masters. For the lineage of the homeland is produced equally <note>original: "ana." A Greek-derived medical term used in prescriptions meaning "of each an equal amount."</note> and similar things are spoken of; the manner of the cultivator’s prophecy is taught, and even first losses happen through the affections of those standing by.

And when the philosophies are spoken of, which each teaches through its own medicinal power; and concerning the healing of sicknesses, it is said to join only the body; through this manner, the truest visible apples are drawn out. What else is the fruit than that which perfects the most powerful medicines? Those choosing from this doctrine, holy philosophers, gather two corporeal spirits brought back by God into one, which its Lord leads by hand to the vegetable nomenclature <note>The "vegetable" state in alchemy refers to the stage of growth and fermentation, not necessarily actual plants.</note>. And the healing secrets of the lasting art are taught, purified by those sucking from sacred things. Indeed, the philosophies of the learned concerning **degrees** <note>original: "gradibus." Referring to the four degrees of intensity for qualities like heat, cold, moisture, and dryness in Galenic medicine.</note> and other things, where the wisdom of nature <unclear>amidib'</unclear> <unclear>spake' g'ndunt</unclear> <unclear>cu'm'os</unclear> regarding the healing of <unclear>angular</unclear> things, [the potion] commanding another thing. 

But through divine inspiration, which in [various] ways <unclear>to make</unclear> <unclear>perceives</unclear>, yet excludes the failures. You, however, friends of philosophy, to whom the silent [gift] comes: may the Almighty God make you for this <unclear>work</unclear> or philosophy concerning the cultivation of justice; I pray that they do not show it <note>A common alchemical warning to keep the "Great Work" secret from the uninitiated or the wicked.</note>.

### Of the Radiola of alchemy <note>original: "Radiala alchimie." Likely a diminutive of "radix" (root), referring to the "little root" or the foundational branching path of the alchemical process.</note>

**I** shall speak in the name of the world, for the Radiola of this sacred art is specifically the nourishment <note>original: "aluma," possibly referring to nourishment or a corruption of "alum."</note> and the *calx* <note>term: "calx" (the powdery ash or oxide remaining after a metal or mineral has been burned or "calcined").</note> of metals and stones, and all the properties which, once heated, purify, penetrate, and dissolve the <unclear>heat</unclear> that should be imparted to a man, and it colors. Finally, I shall dissolve that prepared substance into a medicine. Through this and its other half, which is the same as the established substance, I shall then repeat this so that it dissolves quickly. Afterward, the dissolved substance is a coagulum, and then with that, or a part through its own [nature] into a solution, and then two parts coagulate, and I shall also pour back a fourth part of the water, with which I always began our other things in this work, our exalted things properly in <unclear>the height</unclear> with the soap.

# On the Elixir and its Sublimation

At last, place the vessel with the medicine or the <term>elixir</term> in the earth [...] and so on, skillfully repeating the process until it can no longer be changed. Then, over a slow fire in a clean vessel, let it remain until the evaporation ceases. You will then have the <term>elixir</term> coagulated at the bottom of the vessel in the manner of <term>alum</term> <note>original: "lume," likely referring to the crystalline structure of alumen</note>. Then it should be well <unclear>reddened</unclear>; let it have the fire for 12 hours. Afterward, for another 12 or 13 hours, a higher and spiritual fire must be applied.

And so, with God’s gift, you will find at the bottom of the vessel the <term>philosopher’s stone</term> <note>original: "lapis phicos"; shorthand for lapis philosophorum</note> collected together, dissolved, and as a "clean eye" <note>likely referring to the clarity or the "peacock's eye" stage of alchemical transformation</note> which is indeed the Body of the <term>elixir</term> <note>the universal transmuting agent</note>, the mother of the moon <note>original: "m̄r ā̄ lua"; referring to silver</note>.

<margin>Body of the elixir, the mother of silver</margin>
Let it be distilled again well and saved. And let it be prepared with burnt foliated salt <note>purified, flaky salt used as a flux</note> which is called the Master of the Work. And let it be macerated with the water of the "resurrected" things <note>purified spirits or distillates</note> which comes from the first <term>cinnabar</term> <note>mercury sulfide</note>. Put it to dissolve. Then, put two parts of this water to congeal, and let it congeal just as before. And with the aforementioned water of the resurrected things, let it dissolve again and congeal again. And this congealing must be done up to three times. Then, let the foliated Master be dissolved with this water of <term>cinnabar</term> in such quantity as there is of the <term>calx of silver</term> <note>original: "calce argenti"; silver reduced to a fine powder or oxide through heat</note>... that is, let there be 4 parts of the <term>cinnabar</term> water and 4 or 3 parts of the silver calx, and thus foliated. Then let it congeal.

And in this way, two parts of washed hair <note>human hair was used in alchemy for its ammonia and sulfur content</note> assist the work; and again, use <term>arsenic</term> <note>arsenicum</note> that has been sublimated, and the whites of eggs... and let the hair be placed in the <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used for distillation</note> so that from these things a "dome" is made of the arsenic and egg whites. Place this over the dregs and let it be distilled. From this, there remains... from the two salts, let it "drink" the oil, so that afterwards it may be put to dissolve. And when it has been dissolved, it holds the <term>elixir</term>. And with this final water which has been distilled and rectified, let it be macerated most gently according to the aforementioned work. Finally, let it be put into a hollow... and so let it be dissolved with a slow fire. And afterwards, when it appears to be "revivified" <note>brought back to a metallic or active state</note>, let it congeal. And thus the <term>elixir</term> is completed with the help of God, through whom all things are done. Amen.

### On the philosopher's stone, how it is made

<margin>How the philosopher's stone is made</margin>

Take the best <term>calx of silver</term> and let it be macerated with <term>cinnabar</term> as if with salt water, and let it be dissolved and congealed, and dissolved [again].

# On the Stone of Gold

<margin>On the stone of gold</margin>
Then let gold be <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to a powder through heat</note> and mixed with <term>sal ammoniac</term> with the aforesaid [substances]; let it boil. Afterwards, let it be coagulated until it becomes a stone. Then let it be dissolved for the reddened metal and sal ammoniac in very sharp vinegar. Let plates of [metal] be ignited and quenched in the aforesaid vinegar, repeating the process of immersion and quenching until the plates are consumed within the vinegar. Let the salt remain at the bottom until the vinegar is poured off, resulting in a calcination and pulverization. Let it be dried in four parts and ground in one vessel with the powder of dissolved <term>atrament</term> <note>original: "atmeat"; likely atramentum or vitriol</note> for a period of days, moistening it daily until the parts of the atrament are thoroughly dissolved. Afterwards, let it be <unclear>gocoet</unclear> <note>likely "decocted" or boiled</note> and then coagulated so that it becomes a stone.

<margin>Chapter 3</margin>
**Chapter Three concerning the same.** Likewise, you shall moisten enough <unclear>mercury</unclear> with white water; afterwards it will dissolve in a <unclear>dung</unclear> bath <note>original: "balneo fimus"; a gentle, steady heat source provided by fermenting manure</note>. With as much red water added, it will soon dissolve and congeal, and it will take on an alabaster color. One part will change ten of any body into the "Sun" <note>gold</note>.

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the "Great Work," focusing on the preparation of the "Red Stone" or "Red Plate" through the processing of Borax and specific mineral salts. It follows the previous page’s description of dissolving gold and mercury to create a "white material," now moving toward the more potent red stage of transmutation.</meta>

## Another chapter on the same
You shall place crushed **Borax** <note>original: "Boraxm"; a salt mineral used as a flux in alchemy and metalworking</note> to a height of one palm under an **alembic** <note>a classic alchemical still used for distillation</note>. You will distill it, reserving the **water** and the **oil** separately. Afterward, you shall **calcine** <note>original: "calcinabis"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder</note> the dregs with salt water in a furnace until it becomes white. Afterward, you will moisten this with a fourth part of its own oil, grinding it until it dries. Then you will fix it in a flask over a **smoking fire** <note>original: "igne fumi"; a very gentle, indirect heat, often the first stage of heating in a furnace</note>. It will congeal into a **Red Plate**, of which one part transmutes ten parts [of metal].

## Another chapter from the same on the same
You indeed [shall take] this **lime** <note>the powdery residue from calcination</note> with a fourth part of the white [water] until it is dry. Grind it and let it rest peacefully. Afterward, add a part of the water of its oil so that you may dissolve both. It will dissolve into a **reddish color**. Afterward, it will be congealed into the color of a **Hyacinth** <note>original: "Iacinctu"; a reddish-orange gemstone, likely a zircon or garnet in this context</note>, the color having been changed. Finally, its water would be sufficient, and you will moisten it gradually. And you will congeal it again, and again you will moisten it with the white [water] that was given, and you will dissolve it. For it will dissolve into a **deep red**. And as much water as you applied, it was so long admitting the heat. Let it remain dissolving for 40 days. Afterward, you shall congeal it with a smoking fire. It will congeal into a **Red Substance**, one ounce of which transforms ten pounds of **bodies** <note>original: "corpū"; base metals</note> into **gold** <note>original: "solem"; literally "the Sun," the alchemical symbol for gold</note>.

## The recipe for the pearl
Take one hundred <term>mechal</term> <note>original: "mechal"; likely derived from the 'mithqal,' a unit of weight equal to about 4.25 grams</note> of **Beryl**. One hundred of white "heads" <note>likely the pure crystalline tops of mineral formations</note>. And one hundred of <unclear>spaltatynde</unclear> <note>perhaps a variation of asphaltum or bitumen</note>. And one hundred <term>merchalo</term> <note>another variation of the mithqal weight</note> of lime made from it. And twenty merchale of pure [liquid]. And take one merchale of this powder. Afterward, by grinding and <unclear>working</unclear> it into small masses with small horns and <unclear>pissibis</unclear> until it becomes dry. Then you shall place it in a pot in the **furnace** <note>original: "clibano"</note> and cook it with a firm seal. It will become a reddish piece, like a **Red Hyacinth**. It yields to nothing in weight or color, and except for the **diamond**, it cannot be harmed.

## Another chapter on the same
Take fresh **Borax** with a little **alum** <note>a type of astringent mineral salt</note> and place it in boiled water. Afterward, you shall calcine <term>fenestrated</term> bones <note>original: "ossa fegstras"; literally "windowed" bones, likely referring to porous or perforated bone material</note>. That which is made with the alum becomes <unclear>pussubrs</unclear>. Likewise, afterward, the white water of its juice, cooked with distilled salt water, you shall calcine in the furnace.

### Essence of Pearl

### Another way

Then, all its water having been distilled, take the remainder in a joined **alutel** <note>original: "alutel"; a pot used specifically for sublimating solids</note> as is the custom. This same material, taken with an equal amount of the spirit of water and well-ground upon a marble slab, becomes a paste. Do this by distilling and grinding until you find crystalline [substances] which are white and [imbemes]. Press one part of **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "armoniaci"; ammonium chloride, used as a flux in metallurgy and alchemy</note> with **distilled vinegar** <note>original: "aceto sublimato"; vinegar purified through distillation to increase its acidity</note>. Just as you did with the water, temper the **red stone** <note>original: "mo[m] rubēa"; likely referring to the *massa* or *materia* of the philosopher's stone in its red stage</note> with it. Then, place it in an earthenware vessel joined with **philosopher’s lute** <note>original: "luto artificis"; a sealing clay used to make vessels airtight during heating</note> and put it into the melting furnace. Continue until the stone is formed, completing the required stages, and you shall have what you desired.

<margin>A</margin>
**Suited for tinting:** <note>original: "Aptm ad pin[ctura]m"; likely refers to the "projection" or coloring of metals</note> take **orpiment** <note>original: "auripigmentu[m]"; a yellow arsenic sulfide</note> and grind it in water. Distill the water and the oil. With this water, roll the powder of **gold of Christ** <note>original: "au[ri] c[r]i[s]ti"; possibly a term for a specific high-purity gold or a gold-colored pigment</note> over the stone by its weight. Pour over it "good water" <note>original: "aq̄ b[onam]"; a strong solvent like nitric acid or a specific alchemical preparation</note> and dry it. Then, pour over it the water of dissolved sal ammoniac, and let it become a **reddish plate** <note>original: "lanna rubicunda"; a thin sheet or "lamina" of refined material</note> of this kind, which remains stable for as long as you wish.

<margin>E</margin>
**Essence of Pearls.** <note>original: "Ius margaritaru[m]"; "jus" or juice/essence, a preparation intended to dissolve or mimic the properties of pearls</note> Take small precious stones, fine **lapis lazuli** <note>original: "az u[u]l[v]"; likely *azureum vulgare*</note>, two parts of **coral**, and two parts of **crystal**. Afterward, place it in the furnace so that it becomes a stone in the vessel—a red stone.

<margin>A</margin>
**Suited for [fine] hairs:** <note>original: "ad pilos pīcos"; possibly referring to fine brushes for application or a technical term for filamentous crystals</note> wash them in the same way with water and distill the oil. Likewise, distill a second time with water until it is very clear. Afterward, with the oil, congeal the water over it so that it covers it completely. Take **quicksilver** <note>original: "Vargenti"; *vivum argentum* or mercury</note>, **sal ammoniac**, and **calcined sulfur** <note>original: "sulphre calematu[m]"; sulfur that has been heated to remove impurities</note>. Of the good water, you shall make ten times the amount of the other parts. While it is resting in the good water, press it until it dries. Then pound it again and roast it in a flask with fumes, just as the **mercury** <note>original: "merc[ur]ial[is]"</note> is consumed.

<margin>E</margin>
**Essence of Pearls.** Take gold and gold filings along with **calamine** <note>original: "caloma"; a zinc ore</note> and a tenth part of the reddest **coral**, two parts. Add another red substance of equal weight and purest **crystal**, either equal parts or a tenth part of the whole. Add a quantity of the calamine powder. Upon this mercury, place one part of the stone powder in the furnace so the stone may be replaced. And if the gold is finished, draw it out and keep it. Dissolve this powder from the "hairs" in "good water" with an equal amount of **alkahest** <note>original: "alcahe[m]"; the hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving any substance</note>. Then congeal it, and then dissolve it again. Of all these things, dissolve as much as you can with the **reddening spirit** <note>original: "sp[irit]u"</note> and then congeal.

Also, the **7th water** is made thus: through pure alum to be dissolved for 4 days. Then, with that water, all **fugitive spirits** <note>original: "fugitivū spūs"; volatile substances like mercury or sulfur that escape when heated</note> can be retained by imbibing, and so on.

Also, the **9th water** <note>The manuscript appears to misnumber this as the 9th, though it follows the 7th; it may have been intended as the 8th.</note> for the **Red work** <note>original: "ad ruben"; the final stage of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone</note> is made through the water of <unclear>dūmcti</unclear> and **saffron** <note>original: "croti"; likely referring to Crocus Martis (iron oxide) or vegetable saffron</note>, with which waters the Red is thus retained by calcining the spirits.

Also, the **8th water** is made thus: Take **human blood**, such as the blood of a male child, and <unclear>q̄nia</unclear> with eggs; likewise, give it special care and thus distill it through an **alembic** <note>a chemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> in a bath. And this water has every noble power of **congealing and fixing** <note>original: "gelandi et figendi"; turning a liquid or volatile substance into a stable solid</note> sublimated spirits by frequent agitation, and so on.

Also, the **9th water** for the Red work is thus: Take human blood, reduced somewhat over the fire in an **eggshell** <note>original: "cuculla ovoz"; literally the 'hood' or shell of an egg, used as a tiny crucible</note> and the **stone of the abyss** <note>original: "lapidē de abissi"; likely a poetic name for a dark mineral or a specific reagent like vitriol</note>, 2 parts of **sulfur** <note>original: "Kibrit"; Arabic term for sulfur</note>, and of these distill a red water which retains the **ruddy spirit** in the fire; and if they have been calcined in it, etc.

Also, the **10th water** is that which congeals mercury and fixes it. Take **alkali salt** and once it is prepared, place it in a pit for 6 days under **dung** <note>original: "sub fimo"; horse manure was used to provide a gentle, consistent heat for digestion</note>. Distill it once. With this water, culminate <note>reach the final stage of the process</note>, and so on, and do as was said before.

Also, the **11th water** is thus for the same purpose: Take <unclear>su. l. adū.</unclear> and **verdigris** <note>original: "viridē eris"; the green pigment/crust formed on copper</note>, alum, and **tartar** <note>potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking</note>, and <unclear>m de nicola</unclear> which is better than all others. Let it dissolve for 4 days, and with this water imbibe the <unclear>aq̃</unclear> and other species and put them in a place of congealing, etc.

Also, the **12th water** is thus for the Red work: put **sulfur** in an eggshell, and <unclear>hair of arū</unclear> with it; let it sit for a solution for 24 days to dissolve, and then distill it once. With this water, all red spirits are retained through this means.

Thus let the order of the process be as described above. And here end the **12 waters**, which are the best and most powerful of all for the fixation of spirits.

† **In the name of the Lord.** Here begin various ways of **congealing mercury** <note>original: "gelandī mēuriū"; turning liquid quicksilver into a solid</note>, both for the Red and for the White.

The **first way** for the Red is thus: Take **sulfur** and olive oil, and put them in an iron pan. Place it over the fire and make it boil with a slow fire. It should boil with 2 or 3 parts of sulfur and boil with it until it is dissolved in it. Immediately when the sulfur is dissolved in it, add...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from the previous leaf. Having established the "waters" used as reagents, the author now transitions into specific metallurgical recipes for the "fixation" (making a volatile substance stable under heat) and "congealing" (solidifying) of mercury. and a pound of mercury <note>original: "ancharit"; likely a variant of "alkibric" or a specific term for a mercurial substance</note> and let my substance boil thoroughly, and then take it from the fire and let it cool. And you will find it congealed into a red stone. Also, cook my substance with soot and <unclear>en-ma</unclear>. Also, a second way of **fixing** <note>original: "fixandi"; the process of making a volatile substance, like mercury, withstand fire without evaporating</note> it into red:

Take it pulverized in a strong linen cloth and seal the cloth all over with clay <note>original: "luto"; referring to "lute," a sealant paste used to make vessels airtight</note>, and hang it in the upper part of a certain pot; in the bottom of another should be <unclear>alkali ham-adr.</unclear> and salt. And when the mixture is in the proper way with them, close the two vessels well with a seal so that it does not breathe <note>i.e., so that no vapors leak out</note>. And then sublime it for a day with a suitably hot fire, and you will find it congealed and well-reddened, with the help of God. 

Also, a third way of **congealing** <note>original: "gelandi"; turning a liquid into a solid</note> it into white is thus: I do it with lead in its place. Make a sort of box like a globe out of clay, and leave one hole inside through which it can be put in. Then take an iron rod and melt my substance and by pouring it over quickly while hot, lest my substance remain [unmelted]. Then [add] the mercury <note>original: "ancharit"</note> to that hole as you wish and stop up the hole with the same <term>lute of wisdom</term> <note>original: "luto sapie"; a specialized alchemical sealant made of clay, hair, and other binders used to prevent explosions or leaks</note>. And then put these two into a small <term>aludel</term> <note>original: "aluthel"; a pear-shaped pot or condenser used in the sublimation process</note>. First, however, close the aludel just as there are two melted leads in the aludel. And so let it stand for a whole day over the [furnace] hole with a gentle fire; and thus it will be congealed into white.

Also, a fourth way to white is thus: Put the mercury <note>original: "ancharit"</note> into an iron tube. And stop up the hole of the iron tube; and put it into an aludel as was said before. In which there should be melted <term>tin</term> <note>original: "stagnum"</note> mixed with it from above; and let my tube lie so that the tin covers the whole tube. And let it remain on the furnace thus for a whole day with a stronger fire than if it were to be sublimed. Then let it cool overnight, and in the morning you will find it congealed into white, with the help of God. 

Also, [another] 4th way is thus: make a pit in dry earth in the shape of a crucible, and melt two pounds of tin with double mercury. Then next, from the well-judged <unclear>amanchato</unclear> <note>likely an amalgamated or prepared mercurial substance</note>, place lead there and take it out with your fingers, so that it takes the shape of a cup. Then put in as much mercury as you wish.

...and in this way, you will be successful in all things. Likewise, place **cinnabar** <note>original: "cenobrū"; mercury sulfide, often used for its deep red color</note> in our [vessel] upon marble and rub it with the water of the **stone of Mars** <note>original: "lapidis mārss̄"; likely referring to iron sulfate or a similar iron-based compound</note>.
