Afterward, distill it through a water... and then for eight days, refine it most preciously, always making it more subtle with the aforementioned water of the stone of Mars. Then place it under dung again for 21 days. Likewise, dissolve and distill it through wool, and project the **white water**. 

Afterward, prepare a vessel for distillation, and with it, grind the medicine. Then put it in a boiling <term>alcanna</term> <note>an alchemical vessel or furnace, possibly related to the Arabic "al-qannī"</note> like ash over a slow fire for a long cooking process. And when the parts have appeared... distill the vessel; it is a beautiful and most beautiful color. Afterward, do not put another vessel in the projection as you did before. 

And when it has been prepared, distill it for a long time until you have turned the whole thing into a **tincture**. From the **copper** <note>original: "era"</note>, let there remain in the bottom a crystalline copper. Afterward, take all the aforementioned tincture and put it in a short vessel to congeal with a slow fire. Then it will expel a **red oil**, and there will remain a tincture like **blood**. 

Afterward, take the copper and put it to be **calcined** <note>reduced to powder through heat</note>. When it has been well whitened, coat it with the aforementioned ointment. Then dissolve it in a **bath** <note>original: "balneo"; a "Bain-marie" or water bath for gentle heating</note> as you know. Moreover, let it congeal gently... project 3 parts of the medicine upon 1 part of **gold** <note>original: "aurum"</note>. It makes the aforementioned "butter." 

**Another better way:**
Take the oil which you mentioned above; pour it... so that the copper dissolves, then congeal it. From this congealed matter, project 1 part upon 1 part of gold; it makes it soft... Afterward, take one part of this gold and mix four parts of... and raw... most richly. When they have been best mixed, put them in such a copper vessel... and close the top well with **lute** <note>original: "luro"; a clay or cement used to seal alchemical vessels airtight</note> and project it in ash. And give it a lukewarm fire.

For many, looking upon the irregular lives of the doctors of **philosophy** <note>original: "phie" [philosophiae]</note> or their foolish claims, despise its teachings regarding those things which they do not understand; or, because they gain nothing for their own hands from these things, they ascribe this to **natural science** <note>original: "phisice" [physicae]</note> with an even worse impiety. They say that this is not only easy but indeed impossible, concerning which philosophy says that it is found equally in all things. Among those, however, whose eyes have been opened by being worthily moved by this faculty, there are many who act so as not to look upon philosophy with the dignity it deserves, but only moderately, just as natural science scarcely perseveres in mediocre things. For there is more in **knowledge** <note>original: "cognitione"</note> than you might find in a mediocre response. Because when one follows anything with a little affection and concerning philosophy, he is afterwards to be judged much more within himself. Since in this demanding matter no one can reach the highest state by himself or lead others there, yet escaping few things, let him not cease from this action. Indeed, by the highest turn itself, the way and the reason were necessary in no small degree, and in various and more ample ways. And from the **labor of hands** <note>original: "manuum labore"; a reference to the physical practice of alchemy in the laboratory</note> both profit is seen and the recovery of necessities; nor does he need those whom he might wish to live [likewise], the thing itself being known by those perfected in knowledge.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of the "Art" (alchemy) from the previous folio, transitioning into a technical discussion of the "complexions" or elemental constitutions of the primary metals: lead, tin, and iron. It is profitable, in its turn, to be weary from labor and to be destitute of resources, to be deprived of one’s kingdom by the authority of God, having departed from the midst of grace for us; either because you begin to deliberate, or you do not arrange to bring to completion that which you have started. <note>This serves as a moral warning: the alchemist must be prepared for hardship and must possess the spiritual resolve to finish the "Great Work" once it is begun.</note>

### Concerning the complexion of lead

<margin>Concerning the complexion of lead</margin>
Lead <note>original: "Plumbum"</note>, in its surface appearance <note>original: "altitudine", literally 'height', but used here to mean the outer, manifest qualities</note>, is quicksilver <term>quicksilver: "argentum vivum", the volatile metallic principle often equated with Mercury</term> and soft lead, being melancholic, dry, and containing few dregs; it is a sour, harsh iron. In its depth <note>original: "profundo", referring to the inner or hidden essence of the metal</note>, it is clean gold, sanguine <note>The "sanguine" temperament is associated with the element of Air—hot and moist</note>, hot, moist, resonant, sweet-smelling, and masculine in all things. In every way, just as what has passed is well-digested into one thing; it is driven as a level, clear, and moist thing, white, unpressed, yielding, and feminine. In its other side, it is near the bone <note>likely a metaphor for the core or "stony" nature</note>, fiery, gathered, hot and dry, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine; and likewise it is more suitable and better digested than what was said before.

### Concerning the complexion of tin

<margin>Concerning the complexion of tin</margin>
Tin <note>original: "Stannum"</note>, in its surface appearance, is an aquatic quicksilver, level, clear, and moist; it is white, unpressed, yielding, and feminine. In its depth, it is to be prepared as that which is fiery, gathered, hot, and thin; it is red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. In one respect, it is lead like silver, and with a melancholic nature, it is dry and contains little, being sour, perishing, and feminine. In the other side, it is clean gold, sanguine, hot, and moist, resonant and sweet, fragrant, and masculine. And because it is more suitable, it is itself well-digested like pure gold; because it is adapted into another thing, it is one thing on the side and another in the depth, having become silver.

### Concerning the complexion of iron

<margin>Concerning the complexion of iron</margin>
Iron <note>original: "Ferrum"</note>, in its surface appearance, is of the complexion of iron like fire: solid, hot, and dry, and red and bitter.

<meta>This page continues the technical categorization of metals according to their "complexions"—their internal and external elemental balances (hot, cold, moist, dry). Having previously discussed lead, the text now concludes the section on tin and moves into gold, quicksilver, and silver. The scribe employs a recurring structure: defining a metal’s "height" (its outer, manifest properties) versus its "depth" (its hidden, essential core).</meta>

<gloss>tin</gloss>
...is sharp and masculine. In its depth is quicksilver <term>quicksilver: "argentum vivum", the liquid metal we call mercury</term>, like flowing water. It is cold and moist; white, unpressed <note>original: "inspidū", likely meaning not dense or uncompressed</note>, yielding, and feminine. On one side is gold, of a sharp taste, hot and moist, cooked, sweet-smelling, and masculine. On the other side is lead, like the <unclear>black</unclear> and the melancholic <note>The "melancholic" temperament is associated with the element of Earth—cold and dry</note>. It is cold and dry, unclean, sharp, fetid, and feminine.

### Concerning the complexion of gold
<margin>Concerning the complexion of gold</margin>
**G**old <note>original: "Aurum"</note> in its height <note>original: "altitudine", referring to the outer or apparent nature of the metal</note> is fiery tin <term>tin: "stagnum", associated with the planet Jupiter</term>, choleric, solid, dry, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. In its depth <note>original: "profundo", the hidden, inner essence</note> it is white silver, quicksilver, like flowing water, cold and moist, white, unpressed, yielding, and feminine. On one side it is gold of a sharp taste, hot and moist, cooked, sweet and fragrant, and masculine. On the other side it is lead, like silver and like melancholy, cold and dry, unclean, sharp, foul, and feminine.

### Concerning the complexion of quicksilver
<margin>Concerning the complexion of quicksilver</margin>
**Q**uicksilver is, in its height, cold flowing water, cold and moist, white, unpressed, yielding, and feminine. In its depth, it is tin as it is fiery, choleric, hot and dry, red, bitter, sharp, and masculine. On one side it is lead as dry silver, cold and dry, unclean, sharp, fetid, and feminine. On the other side it is gold, sharp, hot and moist, cooked, sweet, fragrant, and masculine.

### Concerning the complexion of silver
<margin>Concerning the complexion of silver</margin>
**S**ilver is, in its height, white and hard lead, like melancholy, cold and dry, sharp, fetid, and feminine. In its depth it is gold of a sharp taste, hot and moist, cooked, sweet, and fragrant.

## On preparing the Elixir of Tin

<margin>elixir of tin</margin>
**A**ccept 2 parts of **tin** <note>original: "stanni"</note> separated... <unclear>s'lo</unclear> <unclear>mira</unclear>... and grind it little by little upon a **porphyry** <term>porphyry</term> <note>a hard volcanic stone used as a slab for grinding chemicals into fine powders</note> and with force it will absorb... and... in another... a certain part of the alembic placed above with its receiver. Let the joints be closed with **Lute of Wisdom** <note>original: "luto ma'r s'pt'"; likely a contraction for "lutum sapientiae," a sealant made of clay, horsehair, and dung used to make vessels airtight</note> so that it does not breathe <note>i.e., so no vapors escape</note>. Sublimate it, and that which rises to the top, return it upon the dregs of the tin, grinding it upon the porphyry. Reiterate this upon it until nothing more sublimes from it, and thus it will be the [lat'us] elixir.

## Preparation of sal ammoniac
<term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: "salis armoniaci"; ammonium chloride, a pungent salt essential in alchemy for its ability to sublimate and act as a flux for metals</note>

Then, test it upon a **glowing plate** <term>lamina ignita</term> <note>A standard test for "fixity": if a substance stays on a red-hot metal plate without smoking or evaporating, it is considered successfully "fixed"</note>; if it resolves itself and does not emit smoke, then it is pure <note>original: "scoy"; perhaps a contraction for "soccio" or a term for a purified state</note>. ### Chapter 3. Concerning the Red Work

<margin>
Note concerning the Red: that sublimated orpiment <note>original: auripigmentum; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note> by itself becomes as buttermilk; the material prepared for the Red changes to give a color that is fixed, stable, and red. Distill vinegar from vitriol <note>original: atamento; used here to refer to metallic sulfates</note> as mentioned above.
</margin>

Take ten pounds of the sharpest vinegar made from white wine and three pounds of prepared moon-matter <note>original: lunate; likely referring to silver or a specific lunar-associated mineral</note>. Mix them and let them infuse for ten days. Then distill the whole of the work into one, and let there be one pound of pulverized red sublimated orpiment. Its redness increases proportionally. Distill the work; it will distill like a white substance approaching acidity. Then return the bitter matter and infuse it, and distill it again, and renew the bitterness. Then it shall become Red like blood <note>original: Ruben sicut sanguis</note>.

These things are told to you so that a color may be made that is never removed; for whenever the Red is stained with vinegar and the stain is renewed, it possesses an equally good, fixed, and stable color. And this Red is a color that does not remove itself from that which has been transformed in this manner. And it is prepared just as you distill vinegar, yet it does not distill as a red liquid. Vinegar distilled from vitriol <note>original: atamento</note>... [the text here describes further refinements]. Note that what produces the Red overcomes all other distillations; these are the water of sublimated tutty <note>original: tucie; an impure zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</note> and "blessed water" <note>original: aque bēdne; likely aqua benedicta, a potent solvent or acid</note> in which the matter can be placed.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical preparations, specifically moving into the "third way" of the process. The previous page detailed the use of Egyptian vitriol and Indian calamine for red coloration; this page refines the process of "rubrification" (turning substances red) through sublimation and the use of "philosophical oil."</meta>

In the third way, it is described as follows: according to the method by which you wish to rubrify <note>original: "rubificare," the alchemical process of turning a substance red, representing the final stage of the "Great Work"</note>, you must soak it and cook it under its own water. Let this impression be made at the beginning, though it may <unclear>bitterly</unclear> <unclear>fail</unclear> at first; but let the work be made red, and afterwards a reddish grade, and finally a deep red, so that it is not <unclear>impure</unclear>. Finally, the spirits for this rubrification must be sublimated <note>purified by heating into vapor and then solidifying</note> and calcined white <note>reduced to a white powder through high heat</note> from a white calcined <unclear>crust</unclear>, and the <unclear>grinding</unclear> <note>original: "turtredura," possibly a corruption of "tritura" meaning grinding or a specific sediment</unclear> into a paste fixes them.

<margin>d</margin>
**Take** of whatever body <note>in alchemy, "body" usually refers to a base metal or a solid mineral substance</note> you wish, having deposited one or two parts. Soak it with its own weight of red water and "fourth water," and the red spirits are sublimated. Afterward, take one or two parts of this red sublimate and soak it with a fourth part of its weight of purified, dissolved, and distilled red vinegar. Let it be sublimated from it so many times until it <unclear>settles</unclear> at the bottom of the vessel. Afterward, let him fill it with philosophical oil <term>oleo philosophico: a highly refined alchemical substance, often a secret solvent or an "oil" extracted from minerals</term> according to the order described above. This is done through the proportion of spirit of wine <term>spiritus vini: alcohol or ethanol, used as a powerful solvent</term> and sulfur in the form of a red sublimate, which is given <unclear>previously</unclear>; it is said of this that it is perfected. None of the spirit of wine should be distilled through an alembic <term>alembic: a classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling flask and a cooling cap with a spout</term>; instead, add it, and finally add its own oil according to the method. This oil is separated from the hidden part of the white stone <term>lapidis albi: the "White Stone" or "White Elixir," a stage of alchemical perfection that precedes the final Red Stone</term>, which has been mercurinated <note>combined or treated with mercury</note>, distilled, and rectified <note>purified through repeated distillation</note> to a red color. Coagulate this mixture; afterward, let the cooked and distilled substance remain.

<margin>
Chapter 7. 10. Concerning Urine
Concerning Urine
</margin>

Collect the urine of young boys <note>original: "puerorum," alchemists frequently specified the urine of prepubescent boys as it was considered pure and "uncorrupted" for chemical use</note> in a glass vessel, and when the vessel is full, leave the urine in it for twenty days. After those days, carefully empty the urine into another vessel so that the dregs <note>original: "fex," meaning the sediment or lees at the bottom</note> residing at the bottom do not pass into the other; repeat this from vessel to vessel until it leaves a residue. Then boil down the urine to a third part of its volume; and then, it is best to distill it through a filter or through an alembic <term>alembicum: a classic alchemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube used for purifying liquids</term>, which is better. Keep the urine that remains over the dregs for your work.

<margin>
Concerning white water
Concerning white water
</margin>

Take a white eye-stone <note>original: "oculum lapidē albū," likely referring to a specific translucent or white mineral like certain types of chalcedony or quartz</note> and grind it when you wish, until it becomes like marrow; leave it to be soaked for a day and night in brine <note>original: "mure," likely *muria*, a concentrated salt solution</note> within a distilling vessel. Seal the middle and put it into another—that is, into an alembic—placed with its receiver for distillation, and white water will distill out like milk.

Take the kernel <note>original: "nucelle," literally a small nut, referring here to the core or processed interior</note> which came out from the white eye-stones with saltpeter <note>original: "salis petro," potassium nitrate, a powerful oxidizing agent</note> placed over it, and make it so that it is reduced to the consistency of marrow... <note>OCR: "lib. .," likely one pound</note>. And in another, in a larger alembic placed with its receiver, a thick red water will distill and somewhat adhere. Then take it and put it to soak in a horse’s belly <note>original: "ventre equi," a common alchemical term for a warm, fermenting pile of horse manure used to provide a steady, gentle heat for digestion</note> for ten days. Afterward, you shall distill the water with <unclear>r</unclear> <unclear>moderate</unclear> <unclear>heat</unclear>.

And if in place of silver you have placed <unclear>mercury</unclear>, **Gold** <note>original: "Aurū"</note> will always be better. And when the methods of pounding are completed, cast from them; if silver was not already made, it will become good **Gold**. And if you have placed the middle portion when they are fixed in the <term>crucible: a melting pot for metals</term> to dissolve, let it remain until it becomes water.

### On the elixir of the Sun and Moon
<note>In alchemy, the "Sun" (Sol) represents Gold, and the "Moon" (Luna) represents Silver.</note>

<margin>On the elixir of the Sun and Moon</margin>
Take therefore, with the help of God, five <unclear>measures</unclear> of white <term>quicksilver: mercury</term>. Dissolve it with double its amount of <term>aqua regia: a highly corrosive mixture of acids, literally 'royal water'</term>. With thirty-two parts of the white [matter], dissolve it a second time through an alembic, and let it "drink" <note>original: "bibe," referring to the process of imbibition where a dry solid absorbs a liquid</note> with twenty parts to one of quicksilver, for... and congealing it white. Again, dissolve and distill it; and let it drink with twenty parts of silver and <term>sublimated sulfur: sulfur purified by heating it into vapor and then back to solid</term>, and congeal the white three or four times. Again dissolve and distill... which is the last step. Then congeal it into a stone. 

And place it in a glass vessel with three [ounces] of the aforementioned philosophic oil. Make it descend as a <term>menstruum: a solvent, often used in alchemy to refer to a transformative liquid</term>. When it descends, let it be worked with a gentle fire for two days and two nights, then with a moderate fire for another two days, and with the strongest fire for one day. Then [place it] in the crucible and break the mass, and the star-like plate <note>original: "lammā ēstollinā," likely referring to a *lamina stellina*, a crystalline structure thought to indicate success</note> which is at the bottom will be in the vessel. Take three [parts] of it and powder it; and place it... over a gentle fire... and drip over it the white philosophic oil... until it melts like wax. Leave it to congeal, and [place] it upon a royal plate...

## Concerning elixirs for silver and gold. Concerning the elixir for gold and silver.

Take 1 ounce of the aforementioned <term>silver filings: limatura argenti</term> and 6 ounces of the aforementioned <term>quicksilver: mercury (argentum vivum)</term> together. Grind them together strongly until the quicksilver "drinks" <note>imbibition: a process where a solid absorbs a liquid</note> the filings and it becomes a living mass <note>original: "bieuu(m)", likely "vivum"</note> so that no filings can be seen. Then wash it vigorously with vinegar and salt, as mentioned before, until the vinegar comes out clean and clear. Then wash away the salt with clear fresh water. First let it be dried, and when it is dried, add the aforementioned <term>arsenic: arsiceni</term> and <term>sulfur: sulphure</term> and 3 ounces of white distilled <term>colcotar: (colltman) a term for iron oxide or vitriol residue</term>. Grind them together strongly until they appear to be a single body. Then it should hold the spirit with 5 ounces of the said <term>sublimated salt: salt purified by vaporizing and re-solidifying</term>, dissolved and rectified through many [distillations] of <term>aluchet: an alchemical term for a white sublimate or soot</term> and alum.
